========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me, I really appreciate your time. Um, well, I hope it's okay that I'm here for this—it might just be normal stress, honestly, I don't want to make a big deal out of nothing. 

But... I guess what finally pushed me to make an appointment is that I've been having a little trouble at work. I manage a clinic, and lately, I can start an EPIC quality report, or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting with every intention of tracking everything perfectly... but within just a few minutes, my mind kind of just... drifts. It's actually a little embarrassing to admit. I'll find myself rereading the exact same numbers over and over, or I'll suddenly realize I completely missed the whole point of the discussion. 

Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm doing my best for my team, and I wasn't sure if this is something you see people for, or if I'm just explaining it poorly?

Oh, um... high school and college? Well... I mean, I made it through, and I got my degree, so I definitely don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything! I really did put in a lot of effort. 

But, um... I guess if I'm being completely honest... yes, it was still a bit of a challenge. I always felt like I had to study twice as long as my friends just to get the same grades. I'd sit down in the library and... well, my mind would just sort of wander off, or I'd realize I'd read the same page three times without absorbing a single word. I don't really know if I can remember that far back with any real clarity, to be honest. \n\nI mean, I know I mentioned my mother helping me with homework later on in elementary school, but... as for when I was very, very little? I don't think anything really stands out. I was just a normal, happy little girl, I think! I definitely wasn't a troublemaker or anything like that. I always wanted to be a good girl for my parents and teachers. \n\nSo, um, no, I don't specifically remember having trouble when I was that young. I hope it's okay that I can't remember that far back? I really wish I could give you a better answer for that one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you were restless, fidgety, or had trouble sitting still? Or did they say you acted without thinking or interrupted a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... restless or fidgety? No, no, I don't think so. I was always very quiet in class! I really didn't want to bother anyone or cause any trouble. \n\nI mean, I might have daydreamed a little bit, like I mentioned, but I definitely wasn't running around the classroom or interrupting the teacher. I was much too shy for that, honestly. I always tried very hard to be polite and wait my turn to speak. I would have been mortified if a teacher had to scold me for acting out or being disruptive. \n\nSo, um, no, I really don't think anyone ever said I was hyperactive or anything like that. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture of what I was like.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were in elementary school, do you remember often losing things like your homework, books, or personal items? Or did your parents or teachers say you were forgetful about things you needed to do?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / medical_or_health_services_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing that I was bright but careless, and my mother had to sit with me while I finished homework because I would lose track of assignments or forget books at school.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... losing things? Well, I mean, I think all kids lose a mitten or a pencil now and then, right? I really tried my best to keep my things nice. 

But, um... I guess... well, yes, I did sometimes have a little trouble keeping track of everything. I'd sometimes leave my spelling book in my desk at school when I needed it for homework, or... or I'd forget that we had an assignment due. My mother was so wonderful, though—she'd actually sit with me at the kitchen table every night to make sure I finished everything. I think she just wanted to make sure I was doing my best.

And, um, I know my teachers liked me, but I do remember them writing on my report cards that I was bright but... well, they used the word "careless" sometimes. Which always made me feel so terrible, because I really wasn't trying to be! I just... things would just sort of slip my mind. 

I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really did want to do well and please my teachers and my parents. Is that the sort of thing you were looking for?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, middle school and high school... I guess I did eventually become more independent? I mean, I really didn't want to keep bothering my mother, you know? She had her own things to do, and I felt like I should be able to handle it myself by then. 

So, um, she didn't sit with me at the kitchen table every single night anymore. But... well, I suppose I did still struggle a little bit with keeping everything straight. I'd try so hard to use my planner, but sometimes I'd just forget to write things down, or I'd lose the syllabus. I think I just had to work a lot harder and longer than the other kids to get the same grades. I'd stay up really late trying to finish papers at the last minute because I couldn't seem to get started earlier. 

And in college, well, I made it through and got my degree! I was so proud of that. But it was definitely... challenging. I didn't have anyone checking on me, so I had to rely on my friends a lot to remind me when things were due or to study with me so I wouldn't get distracted. 

I hope it's okay that I still needed a bit of a push, even when I got older? I really did try my absolute best to manage on my own. I just... I don't want you to think I wasn't trying hard enough to grow up.

Oh, um... acting quickly without thinking? No, no, I don't think so. I really try to be very careful, especially with my job. As a manager, you know, I have to make sure everything is handled properly, and I would hate to make a mistake that affects someone else.

I guess what finally pushed me to make an appointment is that I've been having a little trouble at work. I manage a clinic, and lately, I can start an EPIC quality report, or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting with every intention of tracking everything perfectly... but within just a few minutes, my mind kind of just... drifts. It's actually a little embarrassing to admit. I'll find myself rereading the exact same numbers over and over, or I'll suddenly realize I completely missed the whole point of the discussion. \n\nDoes that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm doing my best for my team, and I wasn't sure if this is something you see people for, or if I'm just explaining it poorly?\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing this trouble concentrating? Was it something that came on suddenly over days or weeks, or has it been a pattern for much longer?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, it definitely wasn't a sudden thing, like overnight or anything. I don't want to make it sound like I just woke up one day and couldn't do my job! \n\nIt's actually... well, I guess I've really been noticing it since I got a promotion. That was about, um, two years and four months ago? I took on a much larger portfolio for the clinics. And I used to have all these little systems to keep myself on track, but with the extra workload, those systems just sort of... got overwhelmed, I guess? \n\nI feel a little bad even saying that, honestly, like maybe I just need to try harder to organize myself or just put in more hours. But yes, it's been a little over two years of this pattern. Am I giving you the right kind of information? I hope I'm not rambling too much.\n\nDoctor: Besides the trouble concentrating, how have your mood and energy levels been over these past two years? Have you noticed any changes in how you feel emotionally or physically?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my mood and energy? I think they've been pretty normal, honestly. I mean, I definitely get a little frustrated with myself when I fall behind on the reports, but I really try to keep a positive attitude for my staff. I don't feel sad or depressed or anything like that. \n\nAnd physically, my energy is fine—maybe just the normal tiredness you'd expect at the end of a long clinic day, you know? I definitely don't want to complain about being tired when everyone at the office works so hard. So, no, nothing major emotionally or physically, just... just the trouble keeping my focus where it needs to be. \n\nDoes that answer what you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture and not leaving anything important out.\n\nDoctor: Before you got promoted two years and four months ago, did you ever have any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks at work or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I really think back... I don't want to make it sound like I've always been a bad student or employee, because I really do try hard! But, um... yes, I suppose it's always been a little bit of a struggle. \n\nEven back in elementary school, I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright, but... maybe a little careless? Which I always felt terrible about. And my mother, bless her, she used to have to sit with me at the kitchen table just to make sure I finished my homework. I would always somehow lose track of what the actual assignment was, or I'd leave my books at school by accident. \n\nI really tried not to do it, it just... happened. I guess I've always had to work a little extra hard to keep everything together. Is that the kind of thing you mean? I hope I'm not oversharing or making it sound worse than it was.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school and college, before you got promoted, were you still having these same kinds of focus and organization difficulties, or did they seem to get better as you got older?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... high school and college? Well... I mean, I made it through, and I got my degree, so I definitely don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything! I really did put in a lot of effort. \n\nBut, um... I guess if I'm being completely honest... yes, it was still a bit of a challenge. I always felt like I had to study twice as long as my friends just to get the same grades. I'd sit down in the library and... well, my mind would just sort of wander off, or I'd realize I'd read the same page three times without absorbing a single word. Like college was just supposed to be hard and overwhelming. I really tried my best to stay on top of it, and I did graduate, so I hope that counts for something. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your very earliest memories—say, around ages 5 or 6—do you remember having trouble paying attention or staying organized even then, or did these difficulties really start later on?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... ages 5 or 6? Gosh, that's really going back, isn't it? Um... I don't really know if I can remember that far back with any real clarity, to be honest. \n\nI mean, I know I mentioned my mother helping me with homework later on in elementary school, but... as for when I was very, very little? I don't think anything really stands out. I was just a normal, happy little girl, I think! I definitely wasn't a troublemaker or anything like that. I always wanted to be a good girl for my parents and teachers. \n\nSo, um, no, I don't specifically remember having trouble when I was that young. I hope it's okay that I can't remember that far back? I really wish I could give you a better answer for that one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you were restless, fidgety, or had trouble sitting still? Or did they say you acted without thinking or interrupted a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... restless or fidgety? No, no, I don't think so. I was always very quiet in class! I really didn't want to bother anyone or cause any trouble. \n\nI mean, I might have daydreamed a little bit, like I mentioned, but I definitely wasn't running around the classroom or interrupting the teacher. I was much too shy for that, honestly. I always tried very hard to be polite and wait my turn to speak. I would have been mortified if a teacher had to scold me for acting out or being disruptive. \n\nSo, um, no, I really don't think anyone ever said I was hyperactive or anything like that. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture of what I was like.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember often losing things like your homework, books, or personal items? Or did your parents or teachers say you were forgetful about things you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing things? Well, I mean, I think all kids lose a mitten or a pencil now and then, right? I really tried my best to keep my things nice. \n\nBut, um... I guess... well, yes, I did sometimes have a little trouble keeping track of everything. I'd sometimes leave my spelling book in my desk at school when I needed it for homework, or... or I'd forget that we had an assignment due. My mother was so wonderful, though—she'd actually sit with me at the kitchen table every night to make sure I finished everything. I think she just wanted to make sure I was doing my best.\n\nAnd, um, I know my teachers liked me, but I do remember them writing on my report cards that I was bright but... well, they used the word \"careless\" sometimes. Which always made me feel so terrible, because I really wasn't trying to be! I just... things would just sort of slip my mind. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I think I just had to work a lot harder and longer than the other kids to get the same grades. But yes, it's been a little over two years of this pattern. Am I giving you the right kind of information? I hope I'm not rambling too much.\n\nDoctor: Besides the trouble concentrating, how have your mood and energy levels been over these past two years? Have you noticed any changes in how you feel emotionally or physically?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my mood and energy? I think they've been pretty normal, honestly. I mean, I definitely get a little frustrated with myself when I fall behind on the reports, but I really try to keep a positive attitude for my staff. I don't feel sad or depressed or anything like that. \n\nAnd physically, my energy is fine—maybe just the normal tiredness you'd expect at the end of a long clinic day, you know? I definitely don't want to complain about being tired when everyone at the office works so hard. So, no, nothing major emotionally or physically, just... just the trouble keeping my focus where it needs to be. \n\nDoes that answer what you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture and not leaving anything important out.\n\nDoctor: Before you got promoted two years and four months ago, did you ever have any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks at work or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I really think back... I don't want to make it sound like I've always been a bad student or employee, because I really do try hard! But, um... yes, I suppose it's always been a little bit of a struggle. \n\nEven back in elementary school, I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright, but... maybe a little careless? Which I always felt terrible about. And my mother, bless her, she used to have to sit with me at the kitchen table just to make sure I finished my homework. I would always somehow lose track of what the actual assignment was, or I'd leave my books at school by accident. \n\nI really tried not to do it, it just... happened. I guess I've always had to work a little extra hard to keep everything together. Is that the kind of thing you mean? I hope I'm not oversharing or making it sound worse than it was.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school and college, before you got promoted, were you still having these same kinds of focus and organization difficulties, or did they seem to get better as you got older?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... high school and college? Well... I mean, I made it through, and I got my degree, so I definitely don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything! I really did put in a lot of effort. \n\nBut, um... I guess if I'm being completely honest... yes, it was still a bit of a challenge. I always felt like I had to study twice as long as my friends just to get the same grades. I'd sit down in the library and... well, my mind would just sort of wander off, or I'd realize I'd read the same page three times without absorbing a single word. And keeping track of syllabuses and deadlines was always... a bit of a project for me. \n\nI kind of thought everyone felt that way, honestly? Like college was just supposed to be hard and overwhelming. I really tried my best to stay on top of it, and I did graduate, so I hope that counts for something. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your very earliest memories—say, around ages 5 or 6—do you remember having trouble paying attention or staying organized even then, or did these difficulties really start later on?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... ages 5 or 6? Gosh, that's really going back, isn't it? Um... I don't really know if I can remember that far back with any real clarity, to be honest. \n\nI mean, I know I mentioned my mother helping me with homework later on in elementary school, but... as for when I was very, very little? I don't think anything really stands out. I was just a normal, happy little girl, I think! I definitely wasn't a troublemaker or anything like that. I always wanted to be a good girl for my parents and teachers. \n\nSo, um, no, I don't specifically remember having trouble when I was that young. I hope it's okay that I can't remember that far back? I really wish I could give you a better answer for that one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you were restless, fidgety, or had trouble sitting still? Or did they say you acted without thinking or interrupted a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... restless or fidgety? No, no, I don't think so. I was always very quiet in class! I really didn't want to bother anyone or cause any trouble. \n\nI mean, I might have daydreamed a little bit, like I mentioned, but I definitely wasn't running around the classroom or interrupting the teacher. I was much too shy for that, honestly. I always tried very hard to be polite and wait my turn to speak. I would have been mortified if a teacher had to scold me for acting out or being disruptive. \n\nSo, um, no, I really don't think anyone ever said I was hyperactive or anything like that. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture of what I was like.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember often losing things like your homework, books, or personal items? Or did your parents or teachers say you were forgetful about things you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing things? Well, I mean, I think all kids lose a mitten or a pencil now and then, right? I really tried my best to keep my things nice. \n\nBut, um... I guess... well, yes, I did sometimes have a little trouble keeping track of everything. I'd sometimes leave my spelling book in my desk at school when I needed it for homework, or... or I'd forget that we had an assignment due. My mother was so wonderful, though—she'd actually sit with me at the kitchen table every night to make sure I finished everything. I think she just wanted to make sure I was doing my best.\n\nAnd, um, I know my teachers liked me, but I do remember them writing on my report cards that I was bright but... well, they used the word \"careless\" sometimes. Which always made me feel so terrible, because I really wasn't trying to be! I just... things would just sort of slip my mind. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really did want to do well and please my teachers and my parents. Is that the sort of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your mother helped you with homework every night in elementary school. Did this kind of support—needing someone to help you stay on track—continue to be necessary throughout middle school, high school, and even into college, or did you eventually become more independent with managing your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, middle school and high school... I guess I did eventually become more independent? I mean, I really didn't want to keep bothering my mother, you know? She had her own things to do, and I felt like I should be able to handle it myself by then. \n\nSo, um, she didn't sit with me at the kitchen table every single night anymore. But... well, I suppose I did still struggle a little bit with keeping everything straight. I'd try so hard to use my planner, but sometimes I'd just forget to write things down, or I'd lose the syllabus. I think I just had to work a lot harder and longer than the other kids to get the same grades. I'd stay up really late trying to finish papers at the last minute because I couldn't seem to get started earlier. \n\nAnd in college, well, I made it through and got my degree! I was so proud of that. But it was definitely... challenging. I didn't have anyone checking on me, so I had to rely on my friends a lot to remind me when things were due or to study with me so I wouldn't get distracted. \n\nI hope it's okay that I still needed a bit of a push, even when I got older? I really did try my absolute best to manage on my own. I just... I don't want you to think I wasn't trying hard enough to grow up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or making decisions, do you find yourself jumping ahead or acting quickly without fully thinking things through first? Or do people ever tell you that you're impulsive?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... acting quickly without thinking? No, no, I don't think so. I really try to be very careful, especially with my job. As a manager, you know, I have to make sure everything is handled properly, and I would hate to make a mistake that affects someone else. \n\nI mean, I suppose everyone has moments where they maybe speak a little too soon or buy something small they didn't strictly need, right? But... no, I wouldn't say I'm impulsive. I usually take a long time to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm doing the right thing. I really don't want to cause any problems or make things difficult for anyone. \n\nSo, um, no, people haven't told me I'm impulsive. I hope that's okay? I just try very hard to be thoughtful about what I do.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your elementary school years when your teachers wrote 'careless' on your report cards and your mom was helping you with homework—do you remember if you were having trouble focusing and organizing things back then, or did the focus problems mainly start later when you got older and had more responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it really was happening back then, too. I... I know we talked about this a little bit already, but yes, even in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but... but careless. I really hated that word because I wanted so much to be a good student for them. \n\nAnd, um, my mother... she really did have to sit with me at the kitchen table while I finished my homework. Not because I was trying to be difficult or anything! Just... I would lose track of what the assignments were, or I'd realize I forgot to bring the right books home from school. She just wanted to make sure I didn't fall behind. \n\nSo, I guess the trouble with focusing and keeping things organized was there when I was little, too. It wasn't just when I got older. I hope it's alright that I'm repeating myself a little bit? I just want to make sure I'm answering your question properly and giving you the right information.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, um... lower grades? Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? But I don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything like that. I really did try my hardest to do well. 

I think... well, because my mom was helping me so much every night, my grades were actually okay for the most part. She made sure everything got turned in eventually. But I do remember feeling... maybe a little embarrassed sometimes? Like when we'd do reading groups or math worksheets in class, and it seemed like everyone else just finished so much faster than I did. I'd still be staring at the first few problems, trying to figure out where to start, and they were already done. 

So, I guess I did struggle a bit to keep up with the other kids in class, even if my report card grades didn't look completely awful. I just... I really wanted the teachers to like me, so I tried to be very quiet and well-behaved, even if I was falling behind on the work. I hope that makes sense? I just didn't want to be a burden to anyone.

I don't think anything really stands out. I was just a normal, happy little girl, I think! I definitely wasn't a troublemaker or anything like that. I always wanted to be a good girl for my parents and teachers. \n\nSo, um, no, I don't specifically remember having trouble when I was that young. I hope it's okay that I can't remember that far back? I really wish I could give you a better answer for that one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you were restless, fidgety, or had trouble sitting still? Or did they say you acted without thinking or interrupted a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... restless or fidgety? No, no, I don't think so. I was always very quiet in class! I really didn't want to bother anyone or cause any trouble. \n\nI mean, I might have daydreamed a little bit, like I mentioned, but I definitely wasn't running around the classroom or interrupting the teacher. I was much too shy for that, honestly. I always tried very hard to be polite and wait my turn to speak. I would have been mortified if a teacher had to scold me for acting out or being disruptive. \n\nSo, um, no, I really don't think anyone ever said I was hyperactive or anything like that. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture of what I was like.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember often losing things like your homework, books, or personal items? Or did your parents or teachers say you were forgetful about things you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing things? Well, I mean, I think all kids lose a mitten or a pencil now and then, right? I really tried my best to keep my things nice. \n\nBut, um... I guess... well, yes, I did sometimes have a little trouble keeping track of everything. I'd sometimes leave my spelling book in my desk at school when I needed it for homework, or... or I'd forget that we had an assignment due. My mother was so wonderful, though—she'd actually sit with me at the kitchen table every night to make sure I finished everything. I think she just wanted to make sure I was doing my best.\n\nAnd, um, I know my teachers liked me, but I do remember them writing on my report cards that I was bright but... well, they used the word \"careless\" sometimes. Which always made me feel so terrible, because I really wasn't trying to be! I just... things would just sort of slip my mind. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really did want to do well and please my teachers and my parents. Is that the sort of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your mother helped you with homework every night in elementary school. Did this kind of support—needing someone to help you stay on track—continue to be necessary throughout middle school, high school, and even into college, or did you eventually become more independent with managing your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, middle school and high school... I guess I did eventually become more independent? I mean, I really didn't want to keep bothering my mother, you know? She had her own things to do, and I felt like I should be able to handle it myself by then. \n\nSo, um, she didn't sit with me at the kitchen table every single night anymore. But... well, I suppose I did still struggle a little bit with keeping everything straight. I'd try so hard to use my planner, but sometimes I'd just forget to write things down, or I'd lose the syllabus. I think I just had to work a lot harder and longer than the other kids to get the same grades. I'd stay up really late trying to finish papers at the last minute because I couldn't seem to get started earlier. \n\nAnd in college, well, I made it through and got my degree! I was so proud of that. But it was definitely... challenging. I didn't have anyone checking on me, so I had to rely on my friends a lot to remind me when things were due or to study with me so I wouldn't get distracted. \n\nI hope it's okay that I still needed a bit of a push, even when I got older? I really did try my absolute best to manage on my own. I just... I don't want you to think I wasn't trying hard enough to grow up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or making decisions, do you find yourself jumping ahead or acting quickly without fully thinking things through first? Or do people ever tell you that you're impulsive?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... acting quickly without thinking? No, no, I don't think so. I really try to be very careful, especially with my job. But... no, I wouldn't say I'm impulsive. I usually take a long time to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm doing the right thing. I really don't want to cause any problems or make things difficult for anyone. \n\nSo, um, no, people haven't told me I'm impulsive. I hope that's okay? I really hated that word because I wanted so much to be a good student for them. \n\nAnd, um, my mother... she really did have to sit with me at the kitchen table while I finished my homework. She just wanted to make sure I didn't fall behind. \n\nSo, I guess the trouble with focusing and keeping things organized was there when I was little, too. It wasn't just when I got older. Gosh, that's really going back, isn't it? Um... I don't really know if I can remember that far back with any real clarity, to be honest. \n\nI mean, I know I mentioned my mother helping me with homework later on in elementary school, but... as for when I was very, very little? I don't think anything really stands out. I was just a normal, happy little girl, I think! I definitely wasn't a troublemaker or anything like that. I always wanted to be a good girl for my parents and teachers. \n\nSo, um, no, I don't specifically remember having trouble when I was that young. I hope it's okay that I can't remember that far back? I really wish I could give you a better answer for that one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you were restless, fidgety, or had trouble sitting still? Or did they say you acted without thinking or interrupted a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... restless or fidgety? No, no, I don't think so. I was always very quiet in class! I really didn't want to bother anyone or cause any trouble. \n\nI mean, I might have daydreamed a little bit, like I mentioned, but I definitely wasn't running around the classroom or interrupting the teacher. I was much too shy for that, honestly. I always tried very hard to be polite and wait my turn to speak. I would have been mortified if a teacher had to scold me for acting out or being disruptive. \n\nSo, um, no, I really don't think anyone ever said I was hyperactive or anything like that. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture of what I was like.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember often losing things like your homework, books, or personal items? Or did your parents or teachers say you were forgetful about things you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing things? Well, I mean, I think all kids lose a mitten or a pencil now and then, right? I really tried my best to keep my things nice. \n\nBut, um... I guess... well, yes, I did sometimes have a little trouble keeping track of everything. I'd sometimes leave my spelling book in my desk at school when I needed it for homework, or... or I'd forget that we had an assignment due. My mother was so wonderful, though—she'd actually sit with me at the kitchen table every night to make sure I finished everything. I think she just wanted to make sure I was doing my best.\n\nAnd, um, I know my teachers liked me, but I do remember them writing on my report cards that I was bright but... well, they used the word \"careless\" sometimes. Which always made me feel so terrible, because I really wasn't trying to be! I just... things would just sort of slip my mind. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really did want to do well and please my teachers and my parents. Is that the sort of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your mother helped you with homework every night in elementary school. Did this kind of support—needing someone to help you stay on track—continue to be necessary throughout middle school, high school, and even into college, or did you eventually become more independent with managing your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, middle school and high school... I guess I did eventually become more independent? I mean, I really didn't want to keep bothering my mother, you know? She had her own things to do, and I felt like I should be able to handle it myself by then. \n\nSo, um, she didn't sit with me at the kitchen table every single night anymore. But... well, I suppose I did still struggle a little bit with keeping everything straight. I'd try so hard to use my planner, but sometimes I'd just forget to write things down, or I'd lose the syllabus. I think I just had to work a lot harder and longer than the other kids to get the same grades. I'd stay up really late trying to finish papers at the last minute because I couldn't seem to get started earlier. \n\nAnd in college, well, I made it through and got my degree! I was so proud of that. But it was definitely... challenging. I didn't have anyone checking on me, so I had to rely on my friends a lot to remind me when things were due or to study with me so I wouldn't get distracted. \n\nI hope it's okay that I still needed a bit of a push, even when I got older? I really did try my absolute best to manage on my own. I just... I don't want you to think I wasn't trying hard enough to grow up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or making decisions, do you find yourself jumping ahead or acting quickly without fully thinking things through first? Or do people ever tell you that you're impulsive?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... acting quickly without thinking? No, no, I don't think so. I really try to be very careful, especially with my job. As a manager, you know, I have to make sure everything is handled properly, and I would hate to make a mistake that affects someone else. \n\nI mean, I suppose everyone has moments where they maybe speak a little too soon or buy something small they didn't strictly need, right? But... no, I wouldn't say I'm impulsive. I usually take a long time to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm doing the right thing. I really don't want to cause any problems or make things difficult for anyone. \n\nSo, um, no, people haven't told me I'm impulsive. I hope that's okay? I just try very hard to be thoughtful about what I do.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your elementary school years when your teachers wrote 'careless' on your report cards and your mom was helping you with homework—do you remember if you were having trouble focusing and organizing things back then, or did the focus problems mainly start later when you got older and had more responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it really was happening back then, too. I... I know we talked about this a little bit already, but yes, even in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but... but careless. I really hated that word because I wanted so much to be a good student for them. \n\nAnd, um, my mother... she really did have to sit with me at the kitchen table while I finished my homework. Not because I was trying to be difficult or anything! Just... I would lose track of what the assignments were, or I'd realize I forgot to bring the right books home from school. She just wanted to make sure I didn't fall behind. \n\nSo, I guess the trouble with focusing and keeping things organized was there when I was little, too. It wasn't just when I got older. I hope it's alright that I'm repeating myself a little bit? I just want to make sure I'm answering your question properly and giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and your teachers were writing 'careless' on your report cards, do you remember if this was causing real problems for you—like getting lower grades than you were capable of, or having trouble keeping up with your classmates?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... lower grades? Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? But I don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything like that. I really did try my hardest to do well. \n\nI think... well, because my mom was helping me so much every night, my grades were actually okay for the most part. She made sure everything got turned in eventually. But I do remember feeling... maybe a little embarrassed sometimes? Like when we'd do reading groups or math worksheets in class, and it seemed like everyone else just finished so much faster than I did. I'd still be staring at the first few problems, trying to figure out where to start, and they were already done. \n\nSo, I guess I did struggle a bit to keep up with the other kids in class, even if my report card grades didn't look completely awful. I just... I really wanted the teachers to like me, so I tried to be very quiet and well-behaved, even if I was falling behind on the work. I hope that makes sense? I just didn't want to be a burden to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Besides school and work, have these focus and organization difficulties caused problems in other areas of your life—like managing your home, paying bills on time, keeping appointments, or maintaining relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, at home I really try my absolute best to keep things running smoothly. I don't want anyone to think I'm just letting everything fall apart! But... I guess if I'm being completely honest, it is starting to affect other people, which is what I'm most ashamed of. \n\nEven though you asked about outside of work, I... I should probably confess that at the clinic, my delays have actually started affecting our reporting deadlines. Some of the other teams have had to wait on me lately to finish my parts, and I just feel terrible about it. I hate being an inconvenience to my coworkers. \n\nAnd, um, in my personal time... well, I've been involved with these volunteer health workshops. I really wanted to be helpful and do my part, but I actually had to step back from leading parts of them recently. I just couldn't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving, and I was so worried I was going to mess it up for the rest of the volunteers. I thought it would be better for the program if I just stepped down rather than let them down. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too incapable... I really do try very hard to keep up with it all, I just don't want to be a burden on anyone else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... how old was I? Well, I think... I mean, I'm pretty sure it was right from the beginning, really. Like, maybe seven or eight? 

I know I mentioned it before, but... yes, even back in those early elementary school grades, my teachers were already writing those little notes on my report cards. The ones saying I was a bright girl but just... careless. I really did want to do well for them, I promise I wasn't trying to be a bad student! 

And my mom... she was so patient. She started sitting with me at the kitchen table pretty early on to help me finish my homework. Just because... well, I'd somehow lose track of what the actual assignments were, or I'd get all the way home and realize I'd forgotten to bring the right books from school. She just wanted to make sure I was doing okay. 

So, I think it was happening pretty much the whole time I was in elementary school. I hope it's okay that I don't remember the exact age it started? I just know it was quite early on.

I hope I'm not rambling too much.\n\nDoctor: Besides the trouble concentrating, how have your mood and energy levels been over these past two years? Have you noticed any changes in how you feel emotionally or physically?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... my mood and energy? I think they've been pretty normal, honestly. I mean, I definitely get a little frustrated with myself when I fall behind on the reports, but I really try to keep a positive attitude for my staff. I don't feel sad or depressed or anything like that. \n\nAnd physically, my energy is fine—maybe just the normal tiredness you'd expect at the end of a long clinic day, you know? I definitely don't want to complain about being tired when everyone at the office works so hard. So, no, nothing major emotionally or physically, just... just the trouble keeping my focus where it needs to be. \n\nDoes that answer what you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture and not leaving anything important out.\n\nDoctor: Before you got promoted two years and four months ago, did you ever have any trouble concentrating or staying focused on tasks at work or in other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I really think back... I don't want to make it sound like I've always been a bad student or employee, because I really do try hard! But, um... yes, I suppose it's always been a little bit of a struggle. \n\nEven back in elementary school, I remember my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright, but... maybe a little careless? Which I always felt terrible about. And my mother, bless her, she used to have to sit with me at the kitchen table just to make sure I finished my homework. I would always somehow lose track of what the actual assignment was, or I'd leave my books at school by accident. \n\nI really tried not to do it, it just... happened. I guess I've always had to work a little extra hard to keep everything together. Is that the kind of thing you mean? I hope I'm not oversharing or making it sound worse than it was.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school and college, before you got promoted, were you still having these same kinds of focus and organization difficulties, or did they seem to get better as you got older?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... high school and college? Well... I mean, I made it through, and I got my degree, so I definitely don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything! I really did put in a lot of effort. \n\nBut, um... I guess if I'm being completely honest... yes, it was still a bit of a challenge. I always felt like I had to study twice as long as my friends just to get the same grades. I'd sit down in the library and... well, my mind would just sort of wander off, or I'd realize I'd read the same page three times without absorbing a single word. And keeping track of syllabuses and deadlines was always... a bit of a project for me. \n\nI kind of thought everyone felt that way, honestly? Like college was just supposed to be hard and overwhelming. I really tried my best to stay on top of it, and I did graduate, so I hope that counts for something. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to your very earliest memories—say, around ages 5 or 6—do you remember having trouble paying attention or staying organized even then, or did these difficulties really start later on?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... ages 5 or 6? Gosh, that's really going back, isn't it? Um... I don't really know if I can remember that far back with any real clarity, to be honest. \n\nI mean, I know I mentioned my mother helping me with homework later on in elementary school, but... as for when I was very, very little? I don't think anything really stands out. I was just a normal, happy little girl, I think! I definitely wasn't a troublemaker or anything like that. I always wanted to be a good girl for my parents and teachers. \n\nSo, um, no, I don't specifically remember having trouble when I was that young. I hope it's okay that I can't remember that far back? I really wish I could give you a better answer for that one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you were restless, fidgety, or had trouble sitting still? Or did they say you acted without thinking or interrupted a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... restless or fidgety? No, no, I don't think so. I was always very quiet in class! I really didn't want to bother anyone or cause any trouble. \n\nI mean, I might have daydreamed a little bit, like I mentioned, but I definitely wasn't running around the classroom or interrupting the teacher. I was much too shy for that, honestly. I always tried very hard to be polite and wait my turn to speak. I would have been mortified if a teacher had to scold me for acting out or being disruptive. \n\nSo, um, no, I really don't think anyone ever said I was hyperactive or anything like that. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture of what I was like.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember often losing things like your homework, books, or personal items? Or did your parents or teachers say you were forgetful about things you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing things? Well, I mean, I think all kids lose a mitten or a pencil now and then, right? I really tried my best to keep my things nice. \n\nBut, um... I guess... well, yes, I did sometimes have a little trouble keeping track of everything. I'd sometimes leave my spelling book in my desk at school when I needed it for homework, or... or I'd forget that we had an assignment due. My mother was so wonderful, though—she'd actually sit with me at the kitchen table every night to make sure I finished everything. I think she just wanted to make sure I was doing my best.\n\nAnd, um, I know my teachers liked me, but I do remember them writing on my report cards that I was bright but... well, they used the word \"careless\" sometimes. Which always made me feel so terrible, because I really wasn't trying to be! I just... things would just sort of slip my mind. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound completely irresponsible. I really did want to do well and please my teachers and my parents. Is that the sort of thing you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your mother helped you with homework every night in elementary school. Did this kind of support—needing someone to help you stay on track—continue to be necessary throughout middle school, high school, and even into college, or did you eventually become more independent with managing your schoolwork?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, middle school and high school... I guess I did eventually become more independent? I mean, I really didn't want to keep bothering my mother, you know? She had her own things to do, and I felt like I should be able to handle it myself by then. \n\nSo, um, she didn't sit with me at the kitchen table every single night anymore. I really did try my absolute best to manage on my own. I just... But... no, I wouldn't say I'm impulsive. I usually take a long time to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm doing the right thing. I really don't want to cause any problems or make things difficult for anyone. \n\nSo, um, no, people haven't told me I'm impulsive. I hope that's okay? I just try very hard to be thoughtful about what I do.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your elementary school years when your teachers wrote 'careless' on your report cards and your mom was helping you with homework—do you remember if you were having trouble focusing and organizing things back then, or did the focus problems mainly start later when you got older and had more responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it really was happening back then, too. She just wanted to make sure I didn't fall behind. \n\nSo, I guess the trouble with focusing and keeping things organized was there when I was little, too. It wasn't just when I got older. Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? But I don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything like that. I really did try my hardest to do well. \n\nI think... well, because my mom was helping me so much every night, my grades were actually okay for the most part. Oh, goodness... first or second grade? Um... I'm really sorry, but my memory gets just a little bit blurry going back quite that far. I want to give you the exact right answer, but I honestly can't remember for sure if the trouble keeping track of my assignments started right at age six, or if it was maybe a little bit later. 

I was always such a quiet, obedient little girl, so I know I wasn't causing any trouble or disruptions in class back then. I really tried to be good! I just... I know for sure the struggles were happening by the time I was seven or eight, like I mentioned, but the very beginning is just a bit hard to pin down. I hope it's alright that my memory is a little fuzzy on the exact grade it started? I really wish I could be more specific for you.

I always wanted to be a good girl for my parents and teachers. \n\nSo, um, no, I don't specifically remember having trouble when I was that young. I hope it's okay that I can't remember that far back? I really wish I could give you a better answer for that one.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your parents ever mention that you were restless, fidgety, or had trouble sitting still? Or did they say you acted without thinking or interrupted a lot?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... restless or fidgety? No, no, I don't think so. I was always very quiet in class! I was much too shy for that, honestly. I always tried very hard to be polite and wait my turn to speak. I would have been mortified if a teacher had to scold me for acting out or being disruptive. \n\nSo, um, no, I really don't think anyone ever said I was hyperactive or anything like that. Or did your parents or teachers say you were forgetful about things you needed to do?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... losing things? I guess I did eventually become more independent? I mean, I really didn't want to keep bothering my mother, you know? I'd try so hard to use my planner, but sometimes I'd just forget to write things down, or I'd lose the syllabus. I really did try my absolute best to manage on my own. I just... I don't want you to think I wasn't trying hard enough to grow up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or making decisions, do you find yourself jumping ahead or acting quickly without fully thinking things through first? But... no, I wouldn't say I'm impulsive. I usually take a long time to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm doing the right thing. I really don't want to cause any problems or make things difficult for anyone. \n\nSo, um, no, people haven't told me I'm impulsive. I hope that's okay? I... I know we talked about this a little bit already, but yes, even in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but... but careless. I really hated that word because I wanted so much to be a good student for them. \n\nAnd, um, my mother... she really did have to sit with me at the kitchen table while I finished my homework. Not because I was trying to be difficult or anything! Just... I would lose track of what the assignments were, or I'd realize I forgot to bring the right books home from school. She just wanted to make sure I didn't fall behind. \n\nSo, I guess the trouble with focusing and keeping things organized was there when I was little, too. It wasn't just when I got older. I hope it's alright that I'm repeating myself a little bit? I just want to make sure I'm answering your question properly and giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and your teachers were writing 'careless' on your report cards, do you remember if this was causing real problems for you—like getting lower grades than you were capable of, or having trouble keeping up with your classmates?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... lower grades? Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? But I don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything like that. I really did try my hardest to do well. \n\nI think... well, because my mom was helping me so much every night, my grades were actually okay for the most part. She made sure everything got turned in eventually. But I do remember feeling... maybe a little embarrassed sometimes? Like when we'd do reading groups or math worksheets in class, and it seemed like everyone else just finished so much faster than I did. I'd still be staring at the first few problems, trying to figure out where to start, and they were already done. \n\nSo, I guess I did struggle a bit to keep up with the other kids in class, even if my report card grades didn't look completely awful. I just... I really wanted the teachers to like me, so I tried to be very quiet and well-behaved, even if I was falling behind on the work. I hope that makes sense? I just didn't want to be a burden to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Besides school and work, have these focus and organization difficulties caused problems in other areas of your life—like managing your home, paying bills on time, keeping appointments, or maintaining relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, at home I really try my absolute best to keep things running smoothly. I don't want anyone to think I'm just letting everything fall apart! But... I guess if I'm being completely honest, it is starting to affect other people, which is what I'm most ashamed of. \n\nEven though you asked about outside of work, I... I hate being an inconvenience to my coworkers. \n\nAnd, um, in my personal time... well, I've been involved with these volunteer health workshops. I really wanted to be helpful and do my part, but I actually had to step back from leading parts of them recently. I just couldn't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving, and I was so worried I was going to mess it up for the rest of the volunteers. I thought it would be better for the program if I just stepped down rather than let them down. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too incapable... I really do try very hard to keep up with it all, I just don't want to be a burden on anyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you think about elementary school, how old were you when your teachers first started writing things like 'careless' on your report cards, or when your mom first started needing to sit with you at the kitchen table to help with homework?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... how old was I? The ones saying I was a bright girl but just... careless. I really did want to do well for them, I promise I wasn't trying to be a bad student! \n\nAnd my mom... she was so patient. I really did try my hardest to do well. \n\nI think... well, because my mom was helping me so much every night, my grades were actually okay for the most part. She made sure everything got turned in eventually. But I do remember feeling... maybe a little embarrassed sometimes? Like when we'd do reading groups or math worksheets in class, and it seemed like everyone else just finished so much faster than I did. I'd still be staring at the first few problems, trying to figure out where to start, and they were already done. \n\nSo, I guess I did struggle a bit to keep up with the other kids in class, even if my report card grades didn't look completely awful. I just... I really wanted the teachers to like me, so I tried to be very quiet and well-behaved, even if I was falling behind on the work. I hope that makes sense? I just didn't want to be a burden to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Besides school and work, have these focus and organization difficulties caused problems in other areas of your life—like managing your home, paying bills on time, keeping appointments, or maintaining relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, at home I really try my absolute best to keep things running smoothly. I really do try very hard to keep up with it all, I just don't want to be a burden on anyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you think about elementary school, how old were you when your teachers first started writing things like 'careless' on your report cards, or when your mom first started needing to sit with you at the kitchen table to help with homework?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... how old was I? She had her own things to do, and I felt like I should be able to handle it myself by then. \n\nSo, um, she didn't sit with me at the kitchen table every single night anymore. But... well, I suppose I did still struggle a little bit with keeping everything straight. I'd try so hard to use my planner, but sometimes I'd just forget to write things down, or I'd lose the syllabus. I think I just had to work a lot harder and longer than the other kids to get the same grades. I'd stay up really late trying to finish papers at the last minute because I couldn't seem to get started earlier. \n\nAnd in college, well, I made it through and got my degree! I was so proud of that. But it was definitely... challenging. I didn't have anyone checking on me, so I had to rely on my friends a lot to remind me when things were due or to study with me so I wouldn't get distracted. \n\nI hope it's okay that I still needed a bit of a push, even when I got older? I really did try my absolute best to manage on my own. I just... I don't want you to think I wasn't trying hard enough to grow up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something or making decisions, do you find yourself jumping ahead or acting quickly without fully thinking things through first? Or do people ever tell you that you're impulsive?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... acting quickly without thinking? No, no, I don't think so. I really try to be very careful, especially with my job. As a manager, you know, I have to make sure everything is handled properly, and I would hate to make a mistake that affects someone else. \n\nI mean, I suppose everyone has moments where they maybe speak a little too soon or buy something small they didn't strictly need, right? But... no, I wouldn't say I'm impulsive. I usually take a long time to make decisions because I want to be absolutely sure I'm doing the right thing. I really don't want to cause any problems or make things difficult for anyone. \n\nSo, um, no, people haven't told me I'm impulsive. I hope that's okay? I just try very hard to be thoughtful about what I do.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your elementary school years when your teachers wrote 'careless' on your report cards and your mom was helping you with homework—do you remember if you were having trouble focusing and organizing things back then, or did the focus problems mainly start later when you got older and had more responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, yes, I suppose it really was happening back then, too. I... I know we talked about this a little bit already, but yes, even in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but... but careless. I really hated that word because I wanted so much to be a good student for them. \n\nAnd, um, my mother... she really did have to sit with me at the kitchen table while I finished my homework. Not because I was trying to be difficult or anything! Just... I would lose track of what the assignments were, or I'd realize I forgot to bring the right books home from school. She just wanted to make sure I didn't fall behind. \n\nSo, I guess the trouble with focusing and keeping things organized was there when I was little, too. It wasn't just when I got older. I hope it's alright that I'm repeating myself a little bit? I just want to make sure I'm answering your question properly and giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and your teachers were writing 'careless' on your report cards, do you remember if this was causing real problems for you—like getting lower grades than you were capable of, or having trouble keeping up with your classmates?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... lower grades? Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? But I don't want to make it sound like I was failing or anything like that. I really did try my hardest to do well. \n\nI think... well, because my mom was helping me so much every night, my grades were actually okay for the most part. She made sure everything got turned in eventually. But I do remember feeling... maybe a little embarrassed sometimes? Like when we'd do reading groups or math worksheets in class, and it seemed like everyone else just finished so much faster than I did. I'd still be staring at the first few problems, trying to figure out where to start, and they were already done. \n\nSo, I guess I did struggle a bit to keep up with the other kids in class, even if my report card grades didn't look completely awful. I just... I really wanted the teachers to like me, so I tried to be very quiet and well-behaved, even if I was falling behind on the work. I hope that makes sense? I just didn't want to be a burden to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Besides school and work, have these focus and organization difficulties caused problems in other areas of your life—like managing your home, paying bills on time, keeping appointments, or maintaining relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, at home I really try my absolute best to keep things running smoothly. I don't want anyone to think I'm just letting everything fall apart! But... I should probably confess that at the clinic, my delays have actually started affecting our reporting deadlines. Some of the other teams have had to wait on me lately to finish my parts, and I just feel terrible about it. I hate being an inconvenience to my coworkers. \n\nAnd, um, in my personal time... well, I've been involved with these volunteer health workshops. I really wanted to be helpful and do my part, but I actually had to step back from leading parts of them recently. I just couldn't reliably keep all the planning pieces moving, and I was so worried I was going to mess it up for the rest of the volunteers. I thought it would be better for the program if I just stepped down rather than let them down. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too incapable... I really do try very hard to keep up with it all, I just don't want to be a burden on anyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you think about elementary school, how old were you when your teachers first started writing things like 'careless' on your report cards, or when your mom first started needing to sit with you at the kitchen table to help with homework?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... how old was I? The ones saying I was a bright girl but just... careless. I really did want to do well for them, I promise I wasn't trying to be a bad student! \n\nAnd my mom... she was so patient. She started sitting with me at the kitchen table pretty early on to help me finish my homework. Just because... well, I'd somehow lose track of what the actual assignments were, or I'd get all the way home and realize I'd forgotten to bring the right books from school. She just wanted to make sure I was doing okay. \n\nSo, I think it was happening pretty much the whole time I was in elementary school. I hope it's okay that I don't remember the exact age it started? I just know it was quite early on.\n\nDoctor: When you think about first grade or second grade—around age 6 or 7—do you remember if you were already having trouble paying attention in class and keeping track of your assignments back then, or did those difficulties start a bit later?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... first or second grade? Um... I'm really sorry, but my memory gets just a little bit blurry going back quite that far. I want to give you the exact right answer, but I honestly can't remember for sure if the trouble keeping track of my assignments started right at age six, or if it was maybe a little bit later. \n\nI was always such a quiet, obedient little girl, so I know I wasn't causing any trouble or disruptions in class back then. I really tried to be good! I just... I know for sure the struggles were happening by the time I was seven or eight, like I mentioned, but the very beginning is just a bit hard to pin down. I hope it's alright that my memory is a little fuzzy on the exact grade it started? I really wish I could be more specific for you.\n\nDoctor: When you were in first or second grade, do you remember your mother or teachers being concerned that you were falling behind the other children in class, or was that worry something that started a bit later?\n\nPatient: Um, no... If my mom or my teachers were concerned about me lagging behind the others, they certainly didn't let me know about it. I honestly don't remember anyone being worried like that until things got a bit more complicated later on. \n\nDoes that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm answering your questions right, I know my memory of being six is just a little unhelpful right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
